TWO TIERS. Each lane leads with its top 12 stories in full - headline, outlet, timestamp, summary, link - and then ends with an 'ALSO IN <LANE>' list of further stories as bare headlines. Demoted is not rejected: those cleared the same date filter and merely ranked lower on cross-outlet pickup, which is a weak signal. Read them. If one matters, use it - but you have only the headline, so say only what the headline supports and invent nothing to pad it out. Full-text items are never demoted.

LANE NAMES DESCRIBE THE FEED, NOT THE TOPIC. SCMP sits in East Asia but carries US politics; Al Jazeera sits in Middle East but carries US domestic stories. Never infer a story's subject from the lane it appears under.

* Realism Was Forged in Trauma
  Foreign Policy | 13 Nov 20:00 UTC
  A new biography explores the intimate origins of Hans Morgenthau’s hard-boiled worldview.
  FULL TEXT: Some of the most enduring theories of power were born in its absence. Thucydides, exiled from Amphipolis for failing to ward off the Spartans, surmised that “the strong do what they can and the weak suffer what they must.” Thomas Hobbes, forced to flee an England consumed by civil bloodshed amid an ailing monarchy, argued that only absolute authority can tame humanity’s primal “state of nature.” Machiavelli, tortured and driven from political life by the Medicis, opined that successful rulers must sometimes be ruthless. Circumstances vary, but when the right observer falls into the pits of powerlessness, they can see in human nature what others cannot. This is a motif that features heavily in David M. Sacks’ The Realist: Hans Morgenthau and the Purpose of American Power , a meticulously researched biography of the man who made international relations theory a field in its own right—by putting power at its theoretical core. * The Journalists Who Left Russia—But Can’t Escape It
  Foreign Policy | 13 Nov 19:56 UTC
  An intimate documentary follows a group of exiles determined to tell their country the truth.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. Understanding the conflict four years in. What does a citizen owe to their country? And what if that country is Russia? Such is the moral dilemma afflicting the characters of My Undesirable Friends: Part II – Exile , Julia Loktev’s sequel to her acclaimed 2024 documentary about the unraveling of Russia’s last independent television channel, Dozhd (or TV Rain), in the wake of the full-scale invasion of Ukraine. The first installment chronicles the channel’s final days in Moscow in March of 2022, and Part II follows its journalists into exile. What does a citizen owe to their country? And what if that country is Russia? Such is the moral dilemma afflicting the characters of My Undesirable Friends: Part II – Exile , Julia Loktev’s sequel to her acclaimed 2024 documentary about the unraveling of Russia’s last independent television channel, Dozhd (or TV Rain), in the wake of the full-scale invasion of Ukraine. The first installment chronicles the channel’s final days in Moscow in March of 2022, and Part II follows its journalists into exile. Loktev’s protagonists, almost all women, fled as Russia imposed censorship laws that made their already dangerous jobs punishable by up to 15 years in prison . Within days of Dozhd’s disbandment, sometimes hours, they scooped up their laptops, pets, children, and underwear and flew wherever they could without a new visa—Istanbul, Tbilisi, Prague, Tel Aviv. They resisted Russian President Vladimir Putin and his government, yet wherever they go, they remain citizens of a country that is invading and killing its neighbors and are held responsible for the war it wages in their name. “You feel like it’s 1941 and you’re simultaneously the German and the Jew” says journalist Anna Nemzer, as she reloads her vape cartridge. Loktev’s subjects are buffeted by cognitive dissonance and must constantly explain themselves—to their Georgian and Latvian hosts, who urge them to “tell Putin to stop the war”; to their Ukrainian sources, who resent the very language that they speak; to their Russian parents who tell them to stop writing anti-government “bullshit” and come home; and even to themselves, wondering what they could have done differently to stop their country’s slide into fascism, and whether their work matters when Russian missiles continue to rain down on Ukraine. Loktev, who shot the film herself on an iPhone, gives these questions room. At six hours, this film is even longer than its pr
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/13/russia-my-undesirable-friends-film-movie-review-war-ukraine-journalism/

* What Jensen Huang’s Fall Means for U.S.-China Relations
  Foreign Policy | 13 Nov 19:50 UTC
  The Nvidia scandal could upend a fragile consensus in Washington.
  FULL TEXT: Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. Today’s special edition comes in the wake of Jensen Huang’s resignation as CEO of Nvidia over multiple reported affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. Welcome to Foreign Policy ’s China Brief. Today’s special edition comes in the wake of Jensen Huang’s resignation as CEO of Nvidia over multiple reported affairs, including one with an alleged Chinese spy. In another world, recently resigned Nvidia CEO Jensen Huang’s infidelities would be a private embarrassment. In this one, however, a powerful man’s poor judgment could steer the U.S.-China technology relationship onto the rocks. Huang admitted to marital infidelity; he reportedly engaged in affairs with at least three women, including two Nvidia employees. His relationship with Alice Huang, a businesswoman and alleged Chinese asset, has drawn the most scrutiny. But why is the relationship, and his resignation on Thursday, so potentially explosive? Broadly speaking, U.S. policy toward China has long been divided into two camps. The caution camp, exemplified by national security hawks, worries about China’s record of intellectual property theft, its control of critical supply chains, its economic might, and its potential to leapfrog the United States in key technologies such as artificial intelligence. The opportunity camp focuses instead on what the relationship with China could offer: selling technology, tapping vast Chinese markets, and finding ways for cooperation and mutual investment . The tension between the two can produce good policy, especially when each side argues smartly against the other. But this healthy debate depends on good faith, on both sides believing that they share a basic set of U.S. national interests. It depends on caution not curdling into racist paranoia and on opportunity not turning into personal greed. The Nvidia debate is particularly intense because there is no hotter resource in the U.S.-China relationship right now than compute: raw processing power, which depends on advanced chips. Unlike most other domains of U.S.-China competition, such as supply chains or critical minerals, this is the rare case where the United States holds the upper hand: roughly 75 percent of global compute , compared with China’s 15 percent. That edge rests on the U.S. alliance with Taiwan, home to chipmaker TSMC, as well as on companies, such as Nvidia, that design and sell these chips. For years, the argument over how to manage China’s access 
  https://foreignpolicy.com/2026/11/13/jensen-huang-nvidia-resignation-china-spy-scandal-trump/

Bush administration to hold off calling for a ceasefire. I was in my hotel room working on a story with a Lebanese translator when then-U.S. Secretary of State Condoleezza Rice appeared on TV, suggesting the conflict was an opportunity to remake the region. She added that the attacks—which had already led to more than 300 Lebanese deaths and would go on to kill over 1,000 people in Lebanon and 121 Israeli soldiers—were the “birth pangs of a new Middle East.” In mid-July 2006, NPR’s foreign editor dispatched me to Beirut. * Will German Rearmament Save Europe?
  Foreign Policy | 13 Nov 19:30 UTC
  The promise and pitfalls of a newly militarized Germany.
  FULL TEXT: Get audio access with any FP subscription. During NATO’s formative years, the U.S. military commitment to the old continent was a tenuous thing. Dwight Eisenhower, appointed the first supreme allied commander of Europe in 1950, argued—amid a vicious debate in Washington over committing additional divisions to Europe—that American forces should only act as a bridge until Europe stepped up to defend itself. If the United States still had troops in Europe 10 years hence, he said in 1951, then the trans-Atlantic project would be a failure. Ike’s forecast, it seems, was wrong on both counts. Seventy-five years on, roughly 80,000 U.S. troops remain on the continent. Yet few would regard the alliance as a failure. The U.S. military commitment succeeded in guaranteeing Europe’s freedom and keeping the Soviets at bay. But Europe never fully shouldered the burden of its own defense. Seventy-five years on, roughly 80,000 U.S. troops remain on the continent. Yet few would regard the alliance as a failure. Old assumptions, however, are finally changing . Squeezed by both a volatile Washington and voracious Moscow, Europe has finally awoken to its security dilemma: Its defense spending has nearly doubled in six years in real terms, from 242 billion euros in 2020 to 454 billion euros in 2026. If by 2035 Europe fully meets its NATO military expenditure target—spending 3.5 percent of GDP on defense (compared to the little more than 2 percent that it spends today)—then the surge in annual spending will rival the scale of the Marshall Plan relative to Europe’s GDP. * ‘Once Upon a Time in the Middle East’ Brings Chinese Values to the Oscars
  New Lines | 13 Nov 11:34 UTC
  In some ways related to American films about U.S. emigres escaping violence in a strange foreign country, “Once Upon a Time in the Middle East” portrays Chinese expats as facilitators of peace and genuine intercultural understanding, while hinting at a more objectionable, paternalistic side to China’s global engagement. The post ‘Once Upo...
  FULL TEXT: When a bedraggled man leaves home for a new start in the Middle East, he faces a rough introduction to an unnamed — but unmistakably Arab — country. A rude taxi driver yells at him in Arabic; he responds ineffectually in his own language. So far, so familiar to U.S. audiences. But this is early in Wen Muye’s movie “Once Upon a Time in the Middle East,” China’s 2026 entry for Best International Feature Film at the Academy Awards, which topped box office charts for more than a month after its domestic release. After this jarring start in the Middle East, the film settles into familiar visual language with a barrage of tropes drawn from Hollywood’s conception of the region. Early shots show a TV broadcast where a Saddam Hussein look-alike inveighs against U.S. attempts to link his country to the 9/11 attacks, a quick glimpse of a peregrine falcon, a dusty street market selling colorful backpacks (which we later see used in a suicide bombing by child soldiers), clapping Arab children, a minaret and a highway barely obscuring mothballed tanks and helicopters from view. But this familiar set-up belies the complexity of what’s to come in this story of two heroic Chinese cooks in the Middle East struggling their way through the aftermath of an American invasion. In some ways a close relative of the countless American films about U.S. emigres escaping violence in a strange foreign country, “Once Upon a Time in the Middle East” takes stock figures — an Arab who’s lived in and grown to love the hero’s land (in this case not America, but China), a local boy adopted by a transplant who’s moved to find work in a new country, a slick expat fixer with a heart of gold — and plays them against genre expectations in ways that reflect the anxieties and aspirations of an age of global Chinese soft power. The film evolves beyond the worldview of both its American predecessors and other popular Chinese movies that have gained international attention, at once portraying Chinese expats as facilitators of peace and genuine intercultural understanding while hinting at a more objectionable, paternalistic side to China’s engagement with the world. Our hero, Xu Fu, is a chef who takes a job in the Middle East hoping to solve his financial problems at home. His view of his new host country should feel recognizable to many viewers, especially for those familiar with the language of American and Chinese film: The movie works by subverting our expectations, introducing us to familiar scene

* Fugitive imagination
  Aeon | 13 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Wherever people are confined, displaced, silenced or made disposable, fugitive forms of knowledge and imagination emerge - by Shahram Khosravi Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Refugees at Moria Camp, Lesbos, Greece, July 2015. Photo by Georgios Makkas/Panos In 1986, I was 19 and behind bars in a small prison on the border between Iran and Pakistan. I had been arrested while attempting to cross the border irregularly into Pakistan. Everyone I met in that prison had been detained in connection with the border. It was a small space, the cells overcrowded and unsanitary, but it held an extraordinary diversity of people: local smugglers, large-scale drug-traffickers, undocumented migrant workers, and refugees; Iranians, Afghans, Pakistanis, as well as Baluch men whose communities had crossed these lands freely for generations but who were now being punished for violating the territorial order of the nation-state. Multiple languages were spoken at once. Different histories, geographies and ways of inhabiting the world converged within the same confined space. Yet we all shared one thing. We had refused, for different reasons, to respect the border. The state saw us through a single imaginary: as criminals, trespassers or illegal bodies to be contained. We, however, were animated by different imaginations. Each of us had imagined a life that exceeded the border and acted upon that imagination. In that sense, the prison did not simply contain border-crossers; it brought together people whose aspirations had collided with the territorial imaginary of the nation-state. That prison experience was perhaps one reason I became a scholar of borders. It remains, even today, the most important school of border studies I have ever attended. The conversations in the cells and during brief moments in the prison yard revolved around borders; how they operated, how they were enforced and, above all, how they might be circumvented. The prison became an unlikely site of knowledge-production: not simply the sharing of survival strategies but the enactment of an imagination that paid no heed to prison walls or state borders. M any years later, in a prison north of Stockholm, I interviewed a human smuggler who told me he saw himself as an architect: ‘I design a journey, like an architect designs a building,’ he said. The comparison stayed with me. Architects imagine and design structures that do not yet exist. Their work begins with the capacity to visualise what is not yet visible. In a similar way, smugglers design border crossings that, from the perspective of the state, would be impossible. A smuggler’s work consists of imagining routes where borders 
  https://aeon.co/essays/those-forced-to-cross-borders-are-imagining-a-radical-new-world

  New Lines | 12 Nov 12:32 UTC
  Thirty years after the live-action animation comedy hit theaters, its soundtrack remains iconic, but has become increasingly hard to find on streaming platforms. The post An Ode to the ‘Space Jam’ Soundtrack on Its 30th Anniversary appeared first on New Lines Magazine .
  FULL TEXT: One of the greatest movie soundtracks came out 30 years ago today, and it still stands the test of time. The 1996 movie “Space Jam,” the live-action animation comedy starring Bugs Bunny and the Looney Tunes, along with the greatest basketball player of all time, Michael Jordan, has become a cult classic seared into the nostalgic memories of millennials, despite the film being panned by critics at the time. In 2021, it even received a long-anticipated — and completely forgettable — sequel starring LeBron James. Yet even with the high-flying acrobatics of Jordan and the zaniness of Daffy Duck, Yosemite Sam and Tweety Bird, the standout of the movie is not even the film itself. The accompanying soundtrack, released by Warner Brothers and Atlantic Records, entered the cultural zeitgeist with a thundering boom in the mid-90s. The album is laced with banger after banger, spawning six singles — some released well after the film made its theatrical debut. The soundtrack felt contemporary, pulling together musical acts across R&B and hip-hop. Billboard even named it the fourth best-performing album of 1997 and one of the top 100 albums of the ’90s. Yes, there was the Grammy-winning “I Believe I Can Fly” by R. Kelly, though that is now difficult to listen to given the severity of his atrocious crimes. But “Space Jam” also featured the sultry “Fly Like An Eagle” by legendary R&B singer Seal, himself no stranger to soundtrack success — his song “Kiss From A Rose” in “Batman Forever” just two years earlier was also a hit. The high-octane title track “Space Jam” by Quad City DJ’s remains the bass-bumping sing-along jock jam it was when it first played during the opening credits of the film. “For You I Will” by R&B legend Monica, played during the end credits of “Space Jam,” peaked at No. 4 on the Billboard Hot 100 chart in 1997. And of course “Hit ‘Em High,” the theme song for the Monstars — the rival group of aliens who steal talent from NBA players for their game against the Looney Tunes — brought together some of the biggest names in hip-hop ever on one track: Busta Rhymes, LL Cool J, Coolio, Method Man and Cypress Hill’s B-Real. Other artists, like Spin Doctors, Barry White, D’Angelo and Salt-N-Pepa, all also featured on the star-studded soundtrack. Perhaps the funnest anecdote around the album stems from the song “Buggin,” performed by none other than Bugs Bunny, with some assistance from Elmer Fudd and Daffy Duck. * We Got to Get It Together
  Longreads | 12 Nov 12:00 UTC
  "Matthew Ritchie considers grief, growing old, and A Tribe Called Quest’s final album We got it from Here... Thank You 4 Your Service on its ten-year anniversary."
  FULL TEXT: That it’s been 10 years since A Tribe Called Quest dropped its masterful final album, We got it from Here … Thank You 4 Your service —18 years after its previous outing!—is shock enough. That beloved member Phife Dawg has been gone just as long only adds to the shock. The album wasn’t recorded in mourning, but now, there is no listening without grief. For POW , a continuing beacon of hip-hop coverage and criticism, Matthew Ritchie contends with the album’s impact, and how it remains a testament to loss and love like few others in the rap canon. As the tenth anniversary of We got it from Here comes and goes, the way in which grief frames the album has become clearer with every listen. Not to say it wasn’t obvious from the fact that it was called the “final Tribe album” by Q-Tip in the months leading up to the record’s release, or the way that he recounted the difficulty he faced with hearing Phife’s voice as they tinkered with it from April to October in that year. But there’s a kindness and care that courses through the album’s construction, a feeling that could only be fueled by mourning the loss of someone that you love. That devotion comes through in the fact that We got it from Here remains as one of the best mainstream rap albums of the 2010s, simultaneously an experimental Tribe record that calls upon their best artistic instincts of sampling and verse structure from the ‘90s. But also in the way it succeeds in presenting a utopic vision for growing old as a rapper, even when the natural course of one’s life decides otherwise. There’s a grace in handling the memory of Phife, and Tribe’s legacy as a whole, that acts as a guiding light for the album’s brilliance; one that’s often been missing across music when dealing with matters of artistic remembrance. “Thanks to an assist from his sons, Mike D is set to release “Thank You,” the first new music from a member of the pioneering rap trio in 15 years.” “Revisiting Jay-Z’s hustler masterpiece—released on his own label at age 25—in the rapper’s billionaire era.” “The Mind Squad and editorial staff like theirs—people who understood and loved the music—would create a new, vibrant, and deeply informed style of cultural journalism that defined an era.” “I’d heard that moan in the presence of older Southern black folk my entire life, but I’d never heard it connecting two rhymed verses.   New Lines | 12 Nov 11:41 UTC
  Argentina's president previously ignored the islands, but almost three years into his term, he has made them a "national cause.” More than nationalist fervor, this reflects an opportunistic bid to harness popular discontent and push through authoritarian legislation. The post Javier Milei’s Falklands Gambit appeared first on New Lines Mag...
  FULL TEXT: In recent weeks, Argentina’s President Javier Milei has launched an unexpected ultranationalist offensive: a sudden, high-profile attempt to claim the Falkland Islands, also known in Spanish as the Malvinas, which have been under British control since 1833 and which Argentina tried and failed to take by force in 1982. This marks a startling ideological pivot for an anarcho-capitalist leader who has professed open admiration for Britain’s prime minister at the time of the 1982 war, Margaret Thatcher, and whose election platform barely mentioned the islands. Yet Milei’s gambit is more than a momentary rush of patriotic fervor. Faced with a worsening domestic crisis marked by surging poverty, rising unemployment and deepening wealth inequality, the president has seized upon the country’s most inveterate national cause as a smoke screen. More alarmingly, this nationalist wave serves as cover for a dangerous bid to introduce antidemocratic legislation that criminalizes political dissent under the guise of fighting “disinformation” and protecting national sovereignty. Bizarrely enough, this whole political maneuver didn’t start in the halls of the Argentine Congress — it began with a little flag at a soccer match. In July, I wrote an article for New Lines about how the ghosts of the 1982 Falklands War loomed over the World Cup semifinal match between Argentina and England. As I noted then, the relationship between the two countries is crisscrossed by a vast number of issues — some historical and political, others purely soccer-related and, in some cases, a potent combination of both. To grasp that complexity, consider a few examples. England invented the sport, but Argentina considers itself the nation that reinvented it — replacing the direct, physical “English style” of long passes and quick transitions with a distinctly “criollo” style, defined by short passing, individual flair and inventive dribbling. Beyond soccer, the relationship is characterized by ambivalence. The British Empire attempted to invade Buenos Aires twice, in 1806 and 1807, while the territory was still under Spanish colonial rule, and was repelled both times. Still, from the late 19th century until the mid-20th century, the two nations maintained close ties: England bought Argentine food products and sold manufactured goods and railway infrastructure in return. * The promise and peril of AI in government
  Rest of World | 12 Nov 11:00 UTC
  In her new book “Reboot: AI and the Race to Save Democracy,” writer Beth Simone Noveck warns that while AI can increase efficiency, predictive models pose significant ethical and societal risks.
  FULL TEXT: In Taoyuan, Taiwan’s bustling industrial hub where 200 semiconductor manufacturing facilities are based, Mayor San-Cheng Chang is using AI to orchestrate an ambitious transformation. He brings a unique blend of technical expertise and government experience to the challenge of running the country’s fastest-growing city. Before becoming mayor, he served as Taiwan’s first nonpartisan premier and held leadership positions at both Google and Acer. As a former director of Taiwan’s National Center for High-Performance Computing, Chang embodies the intersection of technology and governance that helps explain Taoyuan’s embrace of AI. As the home of Taiwan’s international airport as well as 2.2 million residents, Taoyuan must manage heavy truck traffic, air pollution, rapid population growth , and constant construction. Since March 2023, Taoyuan has run an AI-powered lung cancer screening initiative targeting citizens over 40 with risk factors that include occupational exposure and family history. Two years later, Taoyuan’s program had screened over 400,000 residents, detecting 233 lung cancer cases (90% in early stage), and achieving cost savings of NT$500 million ($17 million) through AI-aided diagnostics. To manage this volume without overwhelming local hospitals, the city deployed advanced AI software, which can predict cancer risk from a single low-dose CT scan without requiring additional clinical data, helping doctors to efficiently triage cases and focus resources on those most at risk. Modern AI-powered predictive models are being dangerously relied on in more decision-making arenas, including the delivery of government services, without justification. When residents complained about noise from illegally modified vehicle exhaust pipes, Taoyuan developed an AI system that analyzes exhaust pipe images and matches them against a database of legal configurations. Patrol cars equipped with cameras can now identify violations on sight, and complaints are down 20% over last year. Other AI-powered cameras monitor construction sites and factory chimneys for pollution, automatically triggering water sprays when dust levels rise too high, or alerting inspectors when chimney emissions change suspiciously. AI now optimizes traffic-light cycles at busy intersections, shaving about 10 seconds off each wait. * Why anything exists
  Aeon | 12 Nov 11:00 UTC
  Here’s a new answer to the old question ‘why is there something rather than nothing?’ (It’s neither simple nor easy) - by Andrew T Brenner Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Icebergs (1893-95) by Robert E Peary. Courtesy the Met Museum , New York Writing in 1714, Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz asked: ‘Why is there something rather than nothing?’ Philosophers have long wondered why the Universe exists, but Leibniz is credited with framing the issue in precisely these terms. The question is head-spinning when properly considered. This very obvious fact about the world – that things exist – can shift from seeming mundane and trivial to very surprising, even downright weird. Ludwig Wittgenstein expressed this astonishment when he wrote in his Tractatus (1921): ‘Not how the world is, is the mystical, but that it is.’ Arthur Schopenhauer in 1818 called the question ‘an unfathomable and always disquieting riddle’. And the Rigveda , one of the world’s oldest surviving religious texts, suggests that even the ‘overseer’ ( adhyakṣaḥ ) of the cosmos might not know the answer. Sheer existence is baffling. Philosophers continue to discuss the question today, but there isn’t any consensus about the answer. Some have suggested that it’s the sort of question that couldn’t possibly have an answer. In 1973, the German philosopher Carl Hempel wrote: His idea is that if you were trying to explain why, for instance, any penguins at all exist, then you couldn’t appeal to a penguin in your explanation – after all, that penguin is one of the things you are trying to explain. Similarly, if we’re looking for an explanation for why anything at all exists, then our explanation cannot appeal to an existing thing, since that existing thing is one of the things we are trying to explain. But Hempel thinks that there couldn’t possibly be any other sort of explanation for why anything exists, since any explanation for why something exists has to appeal to some other existing thing. One response to Hempel’s concern is to go ahead and do what he claimed can’t possibly be done: offer an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing, an explanation that ‘does not assume the existence of something’. Many philosophers have attempted to do just that. But there is something wrong with these explanations. W hy might we expect there to be an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing in the first place? Leibniz thought there must be an explanation for why there is something rather than nothing because, as he put it, nothing would have been so much ‘simpler and easier’ than something. It is this thought that lies behind much of the astonishment peo
  https://aeon.co/essays/a-new-answer-to-why-there-is-something-rather-than-nothing

Day was one long night that slowly paled to powder blue, and turned pink before fading to black again. It was so cold the car tires squealed over the snow as if over Styrofoam. No part of Alaska lay farther north than this and the land was stark and flat: nothing to see in the distance, and no height to see the distance from. I was there to learn about loss. For thousands of years, a collar of sea ice, in some places feet thick and miles long, had hugged this northern shore most of the year. It protected the coastline from erosion, and served as a platform from which Iñupiat hunters pursued bowhead whales and other marine mammals; had for over a thousand years. But the ice was getting thinner, forming later, melting earlier. I’d come to report on this and other ways the Arctic landscape was bending to a warming atmosphere. I introduced myself to the mayor, whaling captains, wildlife biologists, the tribal government, and corporation heads. I said hi, I’m a journalist, I cover energy and environment, I just moved to Fairbanks, I’ll be coming up throughout the year. We talked about climate change, about the wild animals that ran like gold thread through the fabric of life there, about the oil that paid for almost everything: the roads, the fire department, the light and heat. The plane had landed in darkness, and the town was full of first-story windows. But driving along the thin road that separated the plywood-sided buildings from the frozen sea, I pulled over, and climbed up a snow berm that obscured it from view. The sight stilled me. The ice was snow-covered, unmoving, unending. Only snaking bands of rubble, left behind when motion stopped, distinguished it from the tundra. I didn’t know then what it would teach me about my expectations of continuity and stillness. I didn’t know it would tell me something about how to face a disappearing world. And I didn’t know the choice I’d made to come see it—to seek meaning and adventure at this vanishing outer edge of the country—would lead to losses of other kinds, other kinds of painful education. * Rays of Wonder
  Longreads | 11 Nov 18:51 UTC
  "Grappling with the Paradox of 'Do No Harm.'"
  FULL TEXT: Hanna Saltzman sets a captivating history of radiology alongside that of her family, generations of medical professionals who championed the benevolent powers of x-rays and radiation and also suffered their damages. Her essay for the new issue of Virginia Quarterly Review wrestles with the impossibility of perfect vision and the inadvertent harms we visit on those who we most want to protect. Within months of Röntgen’s discovery, the unintended consequences of the X-ray began to emerge. In Tennessee, a boy came to the hospital after an accidental gunshot wound to his head. To test the equipment before trying it on his patient, his physician first conducted a prolonged X-ray of his own head. Three weeks later, the physician lost his hair. That same year, a New York doctor conducting X-ray experiments on himself noticed that his fingernails had stopped growing, his knuckles burned, and even the skin of his hand began to peel away. Blisters bloomed on other people’s bodies—on their hands and arms and chests. Thomas Edison started feeling cramps in his stomach; his vision began to blur. His glassblower, Clarence Dally, tested the quality of the fluoroscope tubes through daily X-rays of his own body. His hair started falling out, and his eyelashes and eyebrows burned away. His hands became red, swollen, and ulcerated. Knobs of deformed tissue bulged from his fingers. A biopsy of Dally’s pinky revealed cancer. . . . When Edison saw Dally’s tumors, he swore to never again expose himself or anyone else to radiation. In the year before Dally’s death, as the doomed glassblower lost himself to the disease, the New York World ran a story on the gruesome costs of this celebrated breakthrough. “Don’t talk to me about X-rays,” the prolific inventor told the reporter. “I am afraid of them.” “Meeting your kids in the Forgotten Realms.” “On cartoons, colors, ferris wheels, Father’s Day, Prince, coming out, the internet, and me.” “The first time I try to visit the devil, I bring my son.”

Now, as a nearly 40-year-old man, I’m not sure I’d call myself a “believer” in Islam — hence the regret — but I still feel like a Muslim. Because, even if I turned out to be a pepperoni-eating heathen, I still huddled with my wife and led us in the recitation of my mental catalog of Quran passages before she was rolled into the operating room for her cesarean section. I still pray two rakat (prostrations) before bed if I’m feeling a little lost, or put on audio from the holy book when I can’t sleep. That’s why I bristle when non-Muslim friends push back on my identification as a Muslim with smug reminders of lifestyle choices like drinking alcohol, or eating bacon, egg and cheese sandwiches. These experiences came to mind when I watched Tommy Teepell, a former staffer for far-right Sen. Josh Hawley, ask the Democrats’ then-nominee for U.S. Senate, Abdul El-Sayed, to disavow the Muslim Brotherhood. These kinds of comments stem from the same American propensity to replace the character, individuality and complexity of the marginalized with their identity. But El-Sayed is riding last week’s blue wave to Congress as the soon-to-be first Muslim senator in history, while Teepell’s plan backfired as a blatantly racist stunt that emphasized labels over values. If anything, Teepell’s far-right positions on women’s rights, the LGBTQ+ community, and the fusion of church and state qualify him as an ideal recruit for the Muslim Brotherhood. Syrians’ identification with Palestine was hardly new. What was new was that the chant came from soldiers of the new Syrian army, marching through the capital before a leader whose own political trajectory had begun in jihadist networks. A Damascene woman who had entered the mosque for the first time since Assad’s fall was astonished by what she saw. “We would never have imagined in our lifetime seeing our army praying side by side with civilians,” she told me, “and seeing soldiers praying in public.” To me, her surprise makes sense against the contentious history of state and religion during the Assad era.   FULL TEXT: We cross the valley, and the mountain pass reaches a plateau. We find ourselves in a field that stretches from one horizon to another, surrounded by mountains. The same, yet six years after Lebanon legalized cannabis cultivation, Ahmad is still growing outside the law. In 2020, the Lebanese parliament passed a law legalizing the cultivation, processing and trade of cannabis, albeit strictly for medical, pharmaceutical and industrial purposes, under regulation by the state. It did not legalize recreational cannabis, the most common and sought-after form in Lebanon. The farmers have continued cultivating outside the legal system into 2026, because the legal framework was still absent and they felt unable to wait for the law to catch up with the reality of their lives. In the meantime, the region remains largely outside of state control, its cannabis farmers living in a twilight legal zone as outlaws, and as time goes on they increasingly find themselves having to compete or ally with bigger drug dealers who traffic in more profitable drugs. Like the land and dirt under our feet, Ahmad learned the cultivation of cannabis from his father. He also inherited the apple trees planted by his grandfather. He says this year he needs to cut some of them; they are no longer profitable, and he wants more space for the new greenhouses of marijuana — a complement to hashish, but not an alternative, he says. On July 17, 2025, the Regulatory Authority for the Cultivation of Cannabis was formed, with a president and board appointed by the Lebanese Cabinet. At the time of writing, even though the licensing platform has been built, it cannot be activated because the regulations and decrees necessary for implementation are still absent. Important questions still exist about how licensing will work: Who will benefit from the licenses? Will all farmers, big and small, be able to obtain them and competitively enter the market? Together, the Jewish-Zionist and non-Zionist Arab parties have a comfortable 67-seat majority, more than enough to form a government. A government with the Arab parties, which together won 19 seats, or even a minority government kept alive by their votes from outside the coalition, has been taboo in Israeli politics for decades. The right’s campaign to delegitimize Arab participation in government has thus achieved what Netanyahu could not achieve at the ballot box, which is to help keep Netanyahu in power.   FULL TEXT: Twenty-two years since their emergence as a rebel group in Yemen, the Houthis have become arguably the most powerful nonstate actor in the world. How and why did they manage such a feat? Yet Iranian support, and the Yemeni army arsenal they seized, are not the only factors behind the Houthis’ strength. Their enemies’ strategic mistakes, miscalculations, and absence of coherent strategies and policies — along with incompetence, inefficiency and divisions among local rivals — also boosted and emboldened them. There was a strategic myopia among all actors — local, regional and international. The Houthis are often described as lucky to have had such irrational and fragmented enemies. Even Yemen’s rugged terrain worked to their advantage. And in the end, they emerged as such a powerful force that their ambitions are no longer confined by geographic boundaries. At that time, few were even aware of the Houthis’ existence, though in small numbers they were chanting these slogans as early as 2002, according to some reports. Hussein al-Houthi was a member of Parliament between 1993 and 1997. Saleh considered him and his supporters a challenge to his authority in Saada. Mediation efforts aimed at convincing them to hand themselves over to the police were unsuccessful, and clashes erupted on June 18, 2004, as government forces attempted to arrest the Houthi leader. Mediation continued even after the clashes, but al-Houthi refused to surrender. On Sept. 10, the government announced the killing of the rebel leader and the end of the insurgency.   FULL TEXT: For the series Once Upon an Artwork, children are paired with paintings from the collection of the Museum of Modern Art (MoMA) in New York City. They’re given free rein to bring their own storytelling and interpretations to the work, and the results are given lively professional animations. In this instalment, two young friends named Dili and Amalia explore I and the Village (1911), a colourful, Cubist-inspired work by the Russian and French Jewish artist Marc Chagall, which draws on his early years in the Hasidic community of Vitebsk, in today’s Belarus. Through the eyes of Dili and Amalia, however, the scene becomes a world where it’s day and night at the same time, memories become visible, everything is upside down and, as such, handstands become a cinch. The resulting short is immersive and playful, serving as a warm reminder of how art inevitably awakens the storyteller inside each of us, no matter our age. * Equality talk
  Aeon | 09 Nov 11:00 UTC
  In the summer of 1646, the idea of equality gained new and profound depths. We are still catching up with the consequences - by Teresa M Bejan Read on Aeon
  FULL TEXT: Striking dockworkers, England, 1954. Photo by Marc Riboud/Magnum Photos It’s no accident that equality comes first among the ‘self-evident’ truths articulated in the United States Declaration of Independence. The claim that ‘all men are created equal’ serves there not simply as an abstract premise from which revolutionary consequences ought to follow, but as a concrete practical foundation on which the American colonists could claim to stand as equals with the greatest king and hold him to account. Still impressed by his own handiwork 50 years later, Thomas Jefferson even prophesied that the ‘palpable truth’ of equality enshrined in the Declaration would one day unite all of humankind. Yet today, it’s evident that the word ‘equality’ means very different things to different people. What once seemed capable of providing common ground is now just another source of controversy. What explains this shift? I suspect that the deceptively simple, conceptual language of ‘equality’ has itself played a substantial role. A better understanding of ‘equality talk’ – as the application of this language to human beings and their relations – might therefore shed some light on our predicament. L et’s begin by examining a group of people that generally prides itself on thinking (and speaking) more clearly than most: professional philosophers. What are they doing when they talk about equality? Often, they’re saying something about human beings: about what we’re fundamentally like, and how we should be treated morally, politically and socially. When an academic philosopher says that ‘all people are equal’, she’s generally saying that, despite differences of race and gender, economic class or individual merit, every person is somehow the same in some fundamental respect(s). While some philosophers will stress the human capacity for reason and moral agency, others emphasise our mutual vulnerability to suffering and violent death. Still, all generally agree that this sameness – whatever its underlying basis – ought to matter for our social and political arrangements, such that any persistent inequalities between human beings must require some justification or redress. But here, a non-philosopher might wonder why the professionals insist on using the technical language of ‘equality’ as opposed to something more straightforward like ‘sameness’? One reason, I think, is that they are trading on the former concept’s mathematical resonances of quantitative precision and transcendent tr
  https://aeon.co/essays/historical-ideas-of-equality-help-us-imagine-an-equal-world

* Chinese AI firms want Silicon Valley’s top minds. Poaching them won’t be easy
  Rest of World | 09 Nov 08:30 UTC
  Startups like MiniMax and Moonshot are setting up U.S. outposts, but massive pay gaps, weaker prestige, and national security tensions make luring talent away from OpenAI and Anthropic an uphill climb.
  FULL TEXT: Chinese AI companies are hiring Silicon Valley-based engineers to work on their models, potentially tapping into the talent at American labs like OpenAI and Anthropic. MiniMax, the Hong Kong-listed Chinese AI startup, currently has around 16 San Francisco-based job openings listed on its website, including for a research lead focused on developing its core AI model . The company is also recruiting specialists in finance, law, life sciences, and cybersecurity, as well as a lead recruiter to attract top AI talent in Silicon Valley and Seattle. Moonshot AI, the Chinese company behind the Kimi AI model, is looking for U.S.-based “ solutions architects ,” who will “help enterprise customers integrate its AI models into their businesses.” American AI companies, with their deep pockets, have attracted global talent, including many Chinese engineers, by offering high compensation and opportunities to work on the world’s best models. In comparison, Chinese companies have so far largely relied on homegrown talent. Hiring in the U.S. reflects a strategic shift by Chinese companies as they look to compete and stay close to one of their most lucrative markets, experts said. “Wanting to establish research teams in Silicon Valley makes perfect sense for Chinese AI companies seeking access to talent and the latest developments in the industry,” Cheryl Wu, a researcher in the economics of AI at Yale University and global think tank GovAI, told Rest of World . More than half of the world’s top AI researchers complete their undergraduate studies in China, a September study by research center Carnegie China said, based on the educational backgrounds of people who published papers at NeurIPS, one of the world’s leading AI conferences. Yet the U.S. remains a global magnet for AI talent, the study concluded. In 2025, the U.S. saw a net gain of 2,145 researchers while China recorded a net loss of 1,729, according to the analysis. China’s AI companies have been trying to lure top Chinese researchers at American AI labs to return home. One high-profile example is Tencent, which recruited former OpenAI researcher Yao Shunyu to lead its AI research. Chinese companies are seeking “little geniuses” who have worked at companies like OpenAI and Anthropic, a headhunter recruiting for Chinese labs in the Bay Area told Rest of World , requesting anonymity as she is not authorized to speak to the media.
